### Review method and plan
1. **Build the truth map first.** Pin current branch heads, ancestry and unique deltas; map each change to its owning lane/criterion. Separate inherited code, composed trunk behavior and still-unmerged sibling work. Never infer completion from PR prose.
2. **Review lane-by-lane, not PR-by-PR.** L1 through L9 are the architectural units. For each lane, inspect production code, composition/root wiring, tests, prompts and the tracker contract together so incremental PR boundaries cannot hide duplication or regressions.
3. **Apply all eight lenses to every material change.** SOLID, DRY, hexagonal boundaries, KISS, typed agent calls versus heuristic machinery, official FastAPI/Pydantic/LangGraph usage, type-safety regression/improvement/neutral, and repository hygiene.
4. **Treat typed agent calls as first-class typed solutions.** Semantic judgment may be simpler and safer as one structured-output agent call returning a validated Pydantic model/discriminated union. The comparison is not “deterministic = typed, agentic = untyped”; it is whether the simplest solution preserves the required contract. Deterministic invariants such as authorization, identity, ownership and transaction semantics still require deterministic enforcement where the contract demands it.
5. **Verify claims against actual composed consumers.** A helper, port, type or test does not count if the running application never constructs or consumes it. Conversely, do not delete a boundary merely because it has one implementation when it is a real I/O seam or useful substitutability boundary.
6. **Use primary framework documentation.** Check FastAPI dependency/lifespan patterns, Pydantic settings/models/discriminated unions/TypeAdapter usage, and LangGraph state/checkpoint/retry/resume patterns against the versions actually resolved by the repository. Framework advice must map to a concrete code path, not generic style guidance.
7. **Evidence standard for findings.** A requested change needs: owning lane/criterion; pinned commit/file/lines or exact behavior surface; demonstrated failure or architecture cost; why it matters; smallest corrective design; verification required; and type-safety verdict. Distinguish confirmed defect, architecture simplification, unresolved spec fork and unverified suspicion. **Agent-authored PR bodies, comments, rationales, self-reviews and “generated with …” narratives are never evidence for correctness or design quality. For Opus-authored work in particular, ignore the prose explanation when judging the implementation: inspect only what changed, what is composed, what tests genuinely prove, and whether the current Linear contract plus primary framework docs support it.**
8. **Publish in Linear as we go.** Code-local findings go on the relevant Linear review/issue; cross-lane findings go to their architectural owner. Avoid duplicate tickets when an existing criterion or issue already owns the work. Notion gets links and resume state only.
9. **Finish with a recovery handoff, not merely a review summary.** The final Linear initiative update will give a coding agent an ordered “heal the initiative” path: canonical resources, regressions versus unfinished work, repair order, lane-based PR organization, acceptance checks, residual risks and then the remaining path to the v0.3 finish line.

### Learnings to preserve across compaction
- The v0.3 roadmap itself already encodes a simplification mandate in KOD-744. New review findings must test whether that accepted plan was actually composed and preserved; do not create a second generic architecture program.
- Green mypy/pytest alone does not establish type-safety quality or architectural correctness. The repo already uses strict mypy; review must detect invalid-state expansion, stringly boundaries, optional-field state machines and lost type information that still type-check.
- A structured-output agent call can be a type-safety improvement when it removes prose parsing/heuristics and returns an exhaustively handled validated union/model.
- Historical agent justifications and decision logs are valuable only as search indexes for intent, forks and candidate failure modes; they never prove that the resulting implementation is correct or minimal. Do not let a persuasive rationale raise confidence in the code. For Opus-authored changes, deliberately separate the explanation from the diff and judge the diff first.
- PR stack prose can become stale. Current ancestry, code and Linear state outrank descriptions.
- Do not equate “reviewed” with “implemented,” “PR open” with “unfinished,” or “merged” with “Done.” The initiative’s current state rules govern those distinctions.
- Container git/network limitations mean this review currently relies on connector source/diff/check evidence unless a runnable checkout becomes available. Historical green gates must not be represented as freshly reproduced.

**Yes—this is a job I can do, and the repository and Linear workspace are accessible.** The right approach is an **architecture-and-regression review against the v0.3 roadmap**, organized by lanes—not a succession of isolated reviews of every incremental PR.
I have only inspected scope, history, configuration, and the available review surfaces. 
Absolutely—you’re right. **Agentic versus deterministic and type-safe versus untyped are separate axes.** Using an agent does not inherently mean weakening the type contract.
A schema-constrained agent call, validated into a Pydantic model or discriminated union, can expose a typed application boundary. Downstream consumers should receive that validated value—not parse prose, guess which fields exist, or reconstruct the result through heuristics.
That sharpens two parts of the review:
**For #5, the question becomes:** Can this heuristic machinery be replaced by one agent call through the existing orchestration infrastructure, with a well-defined output schema, explicit failure handling, and substantially less code?
**For #7, the question becomes:** Is the contract type-safe end to end—from the declared output schema, through validation and the port’s return type, to exhaustive handling by consumers? A replacement that removes string parsing and returns a discriminated union may be a **type-safety improvement**, not merely a simplification.
The remaining distinction is **structural validity versus semantic correctness**: a well-typed answer can still be wrong, whether produced by an agent or handwritten code. Authorization, ownership, and transactional guarantees need their own enforcement; output typing alone does not establish them.
So the standard is **the simplest implementation that preserves the required contracts**, with typed agent calls treated as first-class solutions—not as an architectural compromise.
**1. Lane-oriented review matches the existing delivery plan**
The Linear project describes delivery as lane fires, with the dependency edges—not a prose ordering—defining execution order. It also distinguishes completion in the branch carrying the work from merging: an open PR does not, by itself, mean that a lane is unfinished.
I found the L1–L9 lane issues covering scope input, organize, planning/walking, tracker state, delivery, scope termination, audit, supervision, and tracker-native fire composition.
**Those are the starting points for review ownership.** Individual commits and acceptance-criterion issues should provide traceability beneath that structure, rather than each becoming an independent delivery unit.
There is an important complication: the GitHub stack mixes historical incremental work with integration and newer branch work. For example, **PR #107 currently targets kodezart/v03-live-ownership, not main**, while its description also discusses the much larger cumulative change against main. Its current head is 55a87a5. Reviewing its immediate PR diff is therefore not equivalent to reviewing the complete v0.3 implementation.
My first substantive step would be to establish **which changes belong to which lane, which are already incorporated into the integration branch, and which remain only on sibling branches**. Only then would I propose consolidating or retargeting PRs. Otherwise, “organize by lanes” could accidentally duplicate inherited work or discard useful history.
**2. There is already an architecture baseline worth preserving**
**KOD-744 is an existing v0.3 architecture-simplification plan**, not merely a vague cleanup ticket. It already covers focused settings, discriminated knowledge transports, removal of unused execution paths, narrower ports, shared mechanics, framework usage, and the replacement of unnecessary scanner machinery. It also distinguishes confirmed findings from refuted and still-unverified observations.
v0.3 architecture simplification plan — typed privacy, focused settings and removal of unused code
That changes the review materially. I would not generate another generic list of SOLID/DRY suggestions. I would determine:
Was the accepted simplification implemented, is it actually used by the composed application, and did subsequent work preserve it or introduce a regression?
The specification also needs scrutiny. KOD-744 explicitly records that an earlier “everything must be configurable” prescription was superseded. A review that follows an obsolete criterion could reintroduce the very complexity you want removed.
v0.3 architecture simplification plan — typed privacy, focused settings and removal of unused code

I would treat “Opus started messing things up” as a hypothesis to investigate—not as the verdict. The evidence should identify the actual changes that weakened types, duplicated mechanisms, changed accepted behavior, or weakened tests, regardless of their author. “Generated with Claude Code” alone does not establish the exact model or prove that a change is bad.
# **How I would cover your eight angles**
<table>
<tr>
<td>**Angle**</td>
<td>**What I would examine**</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>**SOLID**</td>
<td>Whether responsibilities and dependencies are coherent; whether consumers receive the smallest useful interfaces; whether real adapters and test implementations satisfy the same behavioral contract. No new abstraction merely to satisfy a slogan.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>**DRY**</td>
<td>Repeated policy, invariants, validation, retry mechanics, session setup, and resource ownership. I would distinguish genuine duplication from superficially similar code with different semantics.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>**Hexagonal architecture**</td>
<td>Dependency direction through the actual application composition; vendor and HTTP representations leaking into domain logic; services depending on concrete adapters; whether ports describe application needs rather than vendor APIs.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>**KISS**</td>
<td>Whether a smaller solution satisfies the same requirement. This includes unused paths, speculative frameworks, excessive configuration, unnecessary state, forwarding layers, and abstractions that only redistribute complexity.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>**Agent calls versus heuristics**</td>
<td>Whether semantic judgment has been implemented as elaborate parsing, scoring, or rule machinery when an existing agent session with a structured result would suffice. Each replacement proposal should explain what code disappears and what validation remains.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>**Official framework practices**</td>
<td>Compare the implementation with official FastAPI, Pydantic, and LangGraph documentation applicable to the versions resolved in uv.lock. Inspect dependency injection and lifetimes, validation and schemas, graph state, reducers, checkpointing, retries, and resume behavior where actually used.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>**Type safety**</td>
<td>Give each reviewed change a **regression / improvement / neutral** verdict against an explicit baseline. Check domain identities, narrow interfaces, discriminated unions, exhaustive handling, optional-field combinations, unsafe casts, suppressions, stringly typed boundaries, and type information lost during serialization.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>**Repository hygiene**</td>
<td>Whether types, configuration, constants, domain logic, adapters, services, handlers, prompts, and tests have coherent owners. Separation should improve cohesion—not create a global dumping ground or a module for every trivial declaration.</td>
</tr>
</table>
There is already a meaningful typing baseline: the inspected configuration enables **strict mypy**, the Pydantic plugin, typed model initialization, and generally prohibits explicit Any, with a specific configuration-module exception. The type review therefore needs to go beyond “does mypy pass?” and ask whether changes make invalid states representable or discard useful guarantees despite passing the checker.
For your fifth point, I would apply an explicit distinction: **semantic judgment is a candidate for an agent call; ownership, authorization, dependency traversal, identity, and other deterministic invariants are not automatically candidates for replacement by a prompt.** The roadmap itself requires computed planning. The objective is less unnecessary machinery without making deterministic guarantees depend on model judgment.
v0.3 architecture simplification plan — typed privacy, focused settings and removal of unused code

### Framework recheck
- FastAPI API surface is currently **healthy/neutral-positive**: route functions are thin, request/response models are typed, shared `Annotated[..., Depends(...)]` aliases match FastAPI's documented recommendation, and application lifecycle uses the recommended `lifespan` async context manager. Do not manufacture a FastAPI finding here.
- LangGraph node retries use the native `retry_policy` API. Checkpointing has a live consumer (job-status reads checkpoint state), so deleting checkpointers wholesale would be wrong. The nested imperative graph/checkpoint-resume TODO remains a possible simplification area, but no canonical finding yet because checkpoint state is used for observability and the v0.3 process-recovery contract also uses tracker + remote branch.
- Pydantic official docs explicitly recommend discriminated unions for predictable tagged unions; this supports the KOD-744 type finding, but the finding was independently established from the code.

### Review stance — code over agent narrative
The user explicitly tightened the standard on 2026-09-12: this is a critical code-quality review, not a reconciliation exercise. Agent-authored rationale — especially Opus-era explanations, self-justifications, claimed intent, and claimed test meaning — is **non-evidence**. Do not seek harmony with prior agents or preserve a design merely because its author explained it at length. Evaluate only what the change actually did: current code, composed wiring, tests, observable behavior, roadmap contracts, and framework guidance. Prior agent text may be used only to locate code paths or historical hypotheses. If the code is over-engineered, duplicated, poorly typed, mislayered, unused, heuristic where a typed agent call is simpler, or otherwise lower quality, say so and request the smallest corrective design. Code quality and architectural integrity outrank deference to prior implementation rationale.
